Agentic Transformers Provably Learn to Search via Reinforcement Learning
Tong Yang, Yu Huang, Yingbin Liang, Yuejie Chi

Abstract
Tree search is a central abstraction behind many language-agent reasoning and decision-making tasks: agents must explore actions, remember failures, and backtrack toward promising alternatives. Yet, we lack a theoretical understanding of how transformer-based policies acquire such search capabilities from the training dynamics of reinforcement learning (RL). We study this question in a stochastic k k -ary tree environment, where an agentic transformer observes only its trajectory history through interaction and receives a terminal reward for reaching a hidden leaf goal node. We first construct a two-head transformer that implements randomized depth-first search (DFS): one head tracks previous actions, while the other detects failure outcomes and triggers backtracking. We then analyze the training dynamics of policy gradient under a depth-wise curriculum, showing that this same DFS mechanism emerges in stages from sparse reinforcement feedback without expert demonstrations. The resulting policy exhibits depth generalization: after training only on depth- 1 1 and depth- 2 2 trees, it succeeds on deeper full trees. We further show that, under imbalanced goal distributions, discounting the return leads to a ranked DFS policy that prioritizes higher-probability branches. Overall, our results identify a mechanistic normal form for transformer-based search, in which attention heads specialize and cooperate to extract decision-relevant traces from context and convert them into agentic action selection via RL training.



1
Introduction

Tree search is a core computational structure in modern learning agents: they must explore different paths, memorize failed branches, and backtrack towards promising alternatives while searching for a target embedded at the leaf of the tree
(
1
;
14
;
15
;
9
)
. It underlies classical systems such as Monte Carlo tree search in game playing and proof search in automated theorem proving, and has recently resurged as an explicit test-time reasoning mechanism in language agents, such as Tree of Thoughts, planning-based reasoning, Language Agent Tree Search, and search for web agents
(
24
;
4
;
27
;
8
;
16
)
.

In these agentic settings, the search tree is usually not handed to the agent as input. Rather, it is a hidden environment revealed through interaction: each action exposes only local information, and the reward can be highly sparse depending on the hidden goal. As a result, the agent needs to access the entire trajectory to determine the optimal next action.
Reinforcement learning (RL) has been established as an effective approach to train the agent’s policy – parameterized by autoregressive transformers – to search via policy gradient methods, even in the absence of explicit supervision of when to branch or backtrack along the tree.

Despite this practical progress, the theory of how transformers
(
18
)
learn to search remains limited, especially from a training dynamic perspective.
A growing literature shows that transformers can implement or learn graph reasoning tasks, but mostly in supervised or RL settings with static instances, where the graph is specified in the prompt or encoded as a fixed abstraction. The learned reasoning skills therefore confine to the retrieval, reachability, or generation of paths, rather than the exploration of an unknown graph through interactions
(
12
;
19
;
26
;
23
;
11
;
20
)
. It remains unclear how a transformer embodies an algorithmic search policy discovered through policy-gradient training despite nonconvexity. We therefore ask:

Can transformers learn to search over trees via policy-gradient training

when the tree is hidden and must be discovered through stochastic interaction?

Figure 1:
An illustration of the interactive tree-search task studied in this paper, which can be viewed as an abstraction of agentic tasks. The environment is a hidden stochastic
k
-ary tree (with
k=3
in the illustration), and at each step the agent chooses a search action based on its interaction history, without observing the full tree as input.

1.1
Our Contributions

We study this question in a stylized environment featuring interaction with stochastic hidden
k
-ary trees, illustrated in Figure
1
. At the start of each episode, the environment samples a tree with distinct node identities and a hidden leaf set as the target goal. Beginning at the root, the agent at each step observes the current node identity together with a local status label indicating whether the node is internal, a non-goal leaf, or the goal; it then chooses a local action, either moving to a child or backtracking to the parent. The agent receives a positive reward only upon reaching the goal. This stylized setting preserves the core of interactive search while abstracting away the burden of opaque representation learning faced by practical LLM agents.

Solving this task requires the agent to search through the tree based on interactive feedbacks from the environment, most naturally, by implementing a depth-first search (DFS) algorithm. In this paper, we investigate how transformers, as the agent policy, acquire precisely the DFS algorithm through RL training. Our main contributions are as follows.

•

A mechanistic transformer construction for interactive search.

We give an explicit two-head shallow transformer that implements randomized DFS with head specialization: one head records which child actions have already been tried at the current node, while the other detects search outcomes and triggers backtracking. These two heads cooperate to form the search algorithm.

•

An optimization analysis of policy-gradient learning.

We prove that policy-gradient training can induce this search circuit from sparse terminal reward alone, without expert demonstrations or action-level supervision. Under a depth-wise curriculum, the transformer first learns to backtrack from failed leafs and avoid repetitive actions by training on depth-1 trees, and then by further training on depth-2 trees, it learns to compose them and backtrack from internal nodes when their offsprings are all exhausted.

•

Provable depth generalization from shallow training.

We show that training on depth-1 and depth-2 trees suffices for the learned policy to succeed on deeper full trees. This demonstrates that the transformer learns compositional search skills rather than memorizing short trajectories.

•

Extension to imbalanced goal distributions.
We extend the analysis beyond the balanced case and show that, when goal probabilities differ across branches, discounting the returns selects a ranked DFS policy that searches more promising branches first and yields the minimum expected path length among all search policies.

These results isolate a core mechanism behind agentic reasoning: using memory of past interaction to control future exploration under partial observability and sparse feedback. By making this mechanism explicit and provable, our work provides a tractable foundation for understanding memory, credit assignment, and algorithmic generalization in agentic ML systems.

1.2
Related Work

Test-time agentic search with language models.

Tree search is a central tool for sequential decision-making, from Monte Carlo tree search and AlphaGo-style systems to neural theorem proving
(
1
;
14
;
15
;
9
)
. In language agents, search has appeared mainly as an inference-time mechanism: ReAct and Reflexion use interaction histories and feedback
(
25
;
13
)
, while Tree-of-Thoughts, RAP, LATS, and web-agent tree search explicitly explore alternative reasoning or environment branches
(
24
;
4
;
27
;
8
)
. Test-time compute scaling further shows the value of verifier- or reward-guided search
(
16
)
. Together, these works establish exploration, lookahead, and backtracking as useful primitives for language agents.

Transformer mechanisms for structured search and reasoning.

A growing literature of theory studies how transformers represent structured search and reasoning, including graph connectivity and path-finding
(
12
;
19
)
, chain-of-thought
(
23
;
7
)
, and learned graph heuristics
(
26
)
. These works do model aspects of search, but mostly through static instances encoded in the input. In contrast, our setting is interactive: the tree is hidden, observations are local, and the transformer must infer the search state from history.

RL training dynamics for transformer reasoning.

Recent theory studies how RL improves transformer reasoning from outcome-level feedback, including planning on graph abstractions
(
20
)
and curriculum or reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) dynamics
(
2
;
11
;
6
)
.
20
are closest to ours: they analyze policy gradient and Q-learning for language-model planning, modeled as generating paths on a graph. This is a search/planning abstraction, but the environment is a fixed graph and the evolving state is the generated node prefix; process rewards can also check adjacency. We instead study a transformer policy interacting with a hidden stochastic tree, where successful search requires learning when to explore, avoid repeats, and backtrack from local observations.

Notation.

Let
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Denote
A_{:,-1}
or
A(:,-1)
as the last column of matrix
A
.
I_{n}
denotes the identity matrix of size
n\times n
,
1_{m\times n}
,
0_{m\times n}
denote the all-ones matrix and all-zeros matrix of size
m\times n
, respectively. Let
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{p}
represent the
\ell_{p}
norm of a vector. In addition,
g(x)\lesssim f(x)
or
g(x)=O(f(x))
(resp.
g(x)\gtrsim f(x)
or
f(x)=O(g(x))
) means that
g(x)\leq c\cdot f(x)
(resp.
g(x)\geq c\cdot f(x)
) for some constant
c>0
and all
x
in some range;
g(x)\asymp f(x)
means that
g(x)\lesssim f(x)
and
g(x)\gtrsim f(x)
. We write
g(x)=o(f(x))
if
g(x)/f(x)\to 0
in the relevant limit.

2
Agentic Tree Search via Reinforcement Learning

We define the tree search task and the corresponding RL formulation.

2.1
Tree search via RL

Throughout this work, we consider full
k
-ary (
k\geq 2
) trees
{\mathcal{T}}
of depth
l({\mathcal{T}})\in[L]
(
L\geq 2
) with
distinct
nodes, i.e., each node in the tree has either
k
children or no children. The nodes of the trees are generated by sampling randomly from the set
[N]
without replacement, where we assume
N\geq\frac{k^{L+1}-1}{k-1}
to ensure any such
k
-ary tree has distinct nodes.
Given a tree
{\mathcal{T}}
, we choose one of its leaf nodes as the goal node
g({\mathcal{T}})
. The task of the agent is to find the goal node by searching over the tree without knowing its topology a priori.

Environment setup.

We first fix
an
environment distribution

{\mathcal{P}}
over the environment parameters
\Phi=({\mathcal{T}},g({\mathcal{T}}))
. At the beginning of each episode, we sample
\Phi\sim{\mathcal{P}}
independently, and the agent starts from the root node
n_{1}=r({\mathcal{T}})
. At the
h
-th timestep, the agent arrives at node
n_{h}\in[N]
and takes one of the following actions from an action space
{\mathcal{A}}
:

\displaystyle a_{h}\in{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\big\{\texttt{up}\big\}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{\texttt{down}}=\big\{\texttt{up}\big\}\cup\big\{\texttt{down}_{i}:i\in[k]\big\},

(1)

where
up
denotes moving to the parent node and
\texttt{down}_{i}
denotes moving to the
i
-th child node. Actions are
illegal
if: (i)
up
at the root, (ii)
\texttt{down}_{i}
at a leaf, or (iii) the action that has already been queried at
n_{h}
.
On the other hand, if
a_{h}
is legal, the agent deterministically moves to the next node
n_{h+1}
according to
a_{h}
. For
h\geq 1
, the agent also receives observation
o_{h}=(n_{h},c_{h})
, where the label
c_{h}
of
n_{h}
is defined as

\displaystyle c_{h}\in\{0,\times,\checkmark\},\quad c_{h}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }n_{h}\text{ is not a leaf,}\\
\times,&\text{if }n_{h}\text{ is a leaf and }n_{h}\neq g({\mathcal{T}}),\\
\checkmark,&\text{if }n_{h}=g({\mathcal{T}}).\end{cases}\quad

(2)

Consequently, we note that
o_{1}=(n_{1},c_{1})=(r({\mathcal{T}}),0)
. The episode terminates when
c_{h}=\checkmark
or an illegal action is taken.

Markov decision process (MDP).

The episode evolves deterministically given
\Phi
and the agent’s actions, and the maximum length of the episode is set as
H=2N
.
1
1

1

Since in our setting, each edge can be visited at most twice (down/up).
We cast the interaction as a Markov decision process (MDP) across episodes by taking the
history
as the state: we set the state at timestep
h
as

\displaystyle s_{h}\coloneqq(o_{1},a_{1},\dots,o_{h-1},a_{h-1},o_{h}).

(3)

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the set of all possible states,
{\mathcal{S}}_{\checkmark}\in{\mathcal{S}}
denote the set of all success states, i.e., if
c_{h}=\checkmark
, then
s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\checkmark}
. In addition, we introduce an absorbing terminal state
s_{\perp}
. The transition kernel is given by the posterior predictive distribution
(
3
)
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(s_{h+1}\mid s_{h},a_{h})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{o\in{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\Phi\sim{\mathcal{P}}(\cdot|s_{h})}[\mathbbm{1}\{o=O(s_{h},a_{h}\mid\Phi)\}]\mathbbm{1}\{s_{h+1}=(s_{h},a_{h},o)\},\\
&\hskip-72.26999pt\text{if }s_{h}\notin{\mathcal{S}}_{\checkmark},\,\,a_{h}\text{ legal at }s_{h}\\
\mathbbm{1}\{s_{h+1}=s_{\perp}\},&\hskip-72.26999pt\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

where
{\mathcal{O}}
is the set of all possible observations,
O(s_{h},a_{h}\mid\Phi)
is the deterministic observation produced by taking action
a_{h}
in state
s_{h}
given a hidden parameter
\Phi
, and
{\mathcal{P}}(\cdot|s_{h})
denotes the posterior distribution over
\Phi
given
s_{h}
.
The reward obtained after taking action
a_{h}
and transitioning to
s_{h+1}
is
r_{h+1}=\mathbbm{1}\{s_{h+1}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\checkmark}\}
. The objective of the agent is to learn a policy
\pi
that maximizes the expected return, i.e.,

\displaystyle J^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{1}\sim\rho,a_{h}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid s_{h}),\atop s_{h+1}\sim{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\mid s_{h},a_{h}),h\in[H]}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}r_{h+1}\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{\Phi\sim{\mathcal{P}},\pi}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}r_{h+1}\right],

(4)

where
\rho(s)\coloneqq{\mathbb{P}}_{\Phi\sim{\mathcal{P}}}[s_{1}(\Phi)=s]

is the initial state distribution. In addition, let
R(\pi;\Phi)
denote the expected success rate of policy
\pi
in environment
\Phi=({\mathcal{T}},g({\mathcal{T}}))
, and denote

\displaystyle R(\pi;{\mathcal{P}})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\Phi\sim{\mathcal{P}}}\left[R(\pi;\Phi)\right]=J^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})

(5)

as the expected success rate of policy
\pi
on trees sampled from the environment distribution
{\mathcal{P}}
. Therefore, maximizing
J^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})
is equivalent to maximizing the success rate
R(\pi;{\mathcal{P}})
.

2.2
Transformer Architecture

We set our agent as a two-layer transformer-based model, which maps its trajectory observations to a probability distribution over the action space. To formally state our model, we first describe the state and action embeddings, followed by the attention model with simplified parameters for the tractability of analysis.

State and action embeddings.

For each node
i\in[N]
, let
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
denote its token embedding. For the action set, let
v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
denote the embedding of action
\texttt{down}_{i}
for
i\in[k]
, and

v_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
denote the embedding of action
up
.
For the label set, let
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}}
denote the embeddings of labels
0,\times,\checkmark
respectively. For notational convenience, let
\overline{n}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
,
\overline{a}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
and
\overline{c}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}}
denote the embedding of node
n_{h}
, action
a_{h}
, and label
c_{h}
at step
h
, respectively. Given any tree
{\mathcal{T}}
, the state embedding
E^{(h)}=E^{(h)}({\mathcal{T}})
of state
s_{h}
is given (auto-regressively) as

\displaystyle E^{(h)}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}E_{n}^{(h)}\\
E_{a}^{(h)}\\
E_{n^{\prime}}^{(h)}\\
E_{c}^{(h)}\\
\end{pmatrix}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}u_{0}&\overline{n}_{1}&\cdots&\overline{n}_{h-1}\\
v_{0}&\overline{a}_{1}&\cdots&\overline{a}_{h-1}\\
\overline{n}_{1}&\overline{n}_{2}&\cdots&\overline{n}_{h}\\
\overline{c}_{1}&\overline{c}_{2}&\cdots&\overline{c}_{h}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}E^{(h-1)},&\begin{pmatrix}\overline{n}_{h-1}\\
\overline{a}_{h-1}\\
\overline{n}_{h}\\
\overline{c}_{h}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(2d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3})\times h}

(6)

with filler embeddings
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
for the first step.
Note that
E^{(h)}
contains all the information in
s_{h}
in a matrix form, and each column
i
of
E^{(h)}
represents the embedding of the transition tuple
(n_{i-1},a_{i-1},n_{i},c_{i})
at step
i
. Let
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
denote the set of all possible state embeddings.

Transformer architecture and multi-turn reasoning.

Our transformer-based agent maps the state to a policy over the action space, i.e.
\pi_{\theta}:\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}\mapsto\Delta({\mathcal{A}})
. Following standard simplification in theoretical analyses
(
5
;
22
;
10
)
, for an input state embedding
E=[E_{n}^{\top},E_{a}^{\top},E_{n^{\prime}}^{\top},E_{c}^{\top}]^{\top}
, we define a two-head transformer model
f:\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
as

\displaystyle f(E)=P\cdot\texttt{head}_{1}(E)+Q\cdot\texttt{head}_{2}(E)

(7)

where
P\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times d_{2}}
,
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times d_{3}}
, and

\displaystyle\texttt{head}_{1}(E)=E_{a}\cdot\mathsf{softmax}\big(E^{\top}{W_{1}^{KQ}}E\big),\quad\texttt{head}_{2}(E)=E_{c}\cdot\mathsf{softmax}\big(E^{\top}{W_{2}^{KQ}}E\big),

(8)

with the key and query matrices merged into a single matrix
W_{i}^{KQ}
. We further simplify the parameterization by setting

\displaystyle W_{1}^{KQ}=\begin{pmatrix}0_{d_{1}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{2}}&B&0_{d_{1}\times d_{3}}\\
0_{d_{2}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{2}\times d_{2}}&0_{d_{2}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{2}\times d_{3}}\\
0_{d_{1}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{2}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{3}}\\
0_{d_{3}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{3}\times d_{2}}&0_{d_{3}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{3}\times d_{3}}\end{pmatrix},\,W_{2}^{KQ}=\begin{pmatrix}0_{d_{1}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{2}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{3}}\\
0_{d_{2}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{2}\times d_{2}}&0_{d_{2}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{2}\times d_{3}}\\
0_{d_{1}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{2}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{1}\times d_{3}}\\
0_{d_{3}\times d_{1}}&0_{d_{3}\times d_{2}}&0_{d_{3}\times d_{1}}&C\end{pmatrix},

where
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}}
and
C\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}\times d_{3}}
, leading to

f_{\theta}(E):=f(E)=PE_{a}\cdot\mathsf{softmax}\big(E_{n}^{\top}BE_{n^{\prime}}\big)+QE_{c}\cdot\mathsf{softmax}\big(E_{c}^{\top}CE_{c}\big),

(9)

where
\theta\coloneqq\{P,Q,B,C\}
collects the parameters of the transformer. The policy
\pi_{\theta}
is then given by applying softmax to the last column of
f_{\theta}(E)
:

\displaystyle\pi_{\theta}(E)\coloneqq\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid E)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\pi_{\theta}(\texttt{down}_{1}\mid E)\\
\vdots\\
\pi_{\theta}(\texttt{down}_{k}\mid E)\\
\pi_{\theta}(\texttt{up}\mid E)\end{pmatrix}\coloneqq\mathsf{softmax}\left((f_{\theta}(E))_{:,-1}\right)\in\Delta({\mathcal{A}}).

(10)

During inference, at each step
h
, given the state embedding
E^{(h)}
, the agent samples an action
a_{h}
according to

\displaystyle\pi_{\theta}(E^{(h)})=\mathsf{softmax}\left(PE_{a}^{(h)}\mathsf{softmax}\big(E_{n}^{(h)\top}B\overline{n}_{h}\big)+QE_{c}^{(h)}\mathsf{softmax}\big(E_{c}^{(h)\top}C\overline{c}_{h}\big)\right),

(11)

and observes
o_{h+1}=(n_{h+1},c_{h+1})
. The next state embedding
E^{(h+1)}
is then updated auto-regressively following (
6
)
as long as the episode is not terminated, and the agent moves to the next timestep
h+1
.

2.3
Transformer Construction for Random DFS

We collect the embeddings in matrix forms as

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\coloneqq(u_{0},\cdots,u_{N}),\,\quad V\coloneqq(v_{0},\cdots,v_{k+1}),\,\quad Z\coloneqq(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}),

(12)

and define induced matrices
A_{M}

(M\in\{B,C,P,Q\})
as

\displaystyle A_{B}\coloneqq U^{\top}BU\in\mathbb{R}^{(N+1)\times(N+1)},\quad A_{C}\coloneqq Z^{\top}CZ\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},

\displaystyle A_{P}\coloneqq PV\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times(k+2)},\quad A_{Q}\coloneqq QZ\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times 3}.

(13)

We first present a set of matrices
\{B,C,P,Q\}
in Theorem
1
that enable the transformer to perform the search task, thereby clarifying its underlying mechanism. This construction is not unique; our particular choice is intended to align more closely with the training dynamics while having a simple form; see Appendix
C.1
for the proof.

Theorem 1

(Transformer construction for random DFS)
.

Assume
U,V,Z
all have full column rank. Then there exists
\theta=\{B,C,P,Q\}
such that

\displaystyle A_{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&a_{b,0}1_{1\times N}\\
0_{N\times 1}&a_{b,1}I_{N}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad A_{C}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{c,0}&-a_{c,1}&0\\
-a_{c,0}&a_{c,1}&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle A_{P}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0_{k\times 1}&-a_{p,1}I_{k}&0_{k\times 1}\\
0&0_{1\times k}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad A_{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{a_{q,0}}{k}1_{k\times 1}&-\frac{a_{q,\times}}{k}1_{k\times 1}&0_{k\times 1}\\
-a_{q,0}&a_{q,\times}&0\end{pmatrix}

(14)

for any
a_{b,0},a_{b,1},a_{c,0},a_{c,1},a_{p,1},a_{q,0},a_{q,\times}\in\mathbb{R}
. For any
k\geq 2
,
L\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,
N\geq\frac{k^{L+1}-1}{k-1}
and precision
\epsilon>0
, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that when

\displaystyle e^{a_{c,0}}\geq\ln k,\quad e^{a_{c,1}}\geq\sqrt{N}\ln k,\quad e^{a_{b,0}}\geq N\ln k,\quad e^{a_{b,1}}\geq\ln ke^{a_{b,0}},

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p,1}}{k}\asymp a_{q,0}\asymp a_{q,\times},\quad a_{q,0},\,a_{q,\times}\geq C\ln\left(N/\epsilon\right),\quad\frac{a_{p,1}}{k}\geq C\left(\ln\left(N/\epsilon\right)+a_{q,0}\right),

(15)

we have

\displaystyle R(\pi_{\theta};\Phi)\geq 1-\epsilon

(16)

for any
\Phi=({\mathcal{T}},g({\mathcal{T}}))
, where
{\mathcal{T}}
is a full
k
-ary tree with depth
l\in[L]
, and
g({\mathcal{T}})
is an arbitrary leaf of
{\mathcal{T}}
.

Under our construction, the agent performs
random DFS
: it selects randomly among unvisited children, and goes up only at a wrong leaf or after all children of the current node have been explored.
Figure
2
illustrates how the two heads specialize and cooperate to perform random DFS.

•

Head 2, associated with matrices
C
and
Q
, uses label history to promote action
up
at non-goal leaves and suppress
up
at internal nodes.

•

Head 1, associated with matrices
B
and
P
, retrieves action history and suppresses tried children from being selected again.

When all children of an internal node have been tried, every logit associated with
\texttt{down}_{i}
is suppressed by Head 1, then the logic associated with
up
becomes largest despite Head 2’s internal-node bias, pushing agent to backtrack from an unsuccessful branch.

Figure 2:
Illustration of the transformer mechanism implementing random DFS on a full 3-ary tree. Blue and yellow shading indicate the attention associations of heads 1 and 2, respectively.

3
Training Dynamics and Generalization of Transformers

We begin with specifying the environment distribution that is used for policy gradient training, followed by the training dynamic and generalization performance of our transformer policy.

3.1
Training Environment and Policy Gradient

Training distribution.

To describe the training distribution
{\mathcal{P}}_{l}
for any fixed depth
l\in[L]
, we define the training tree set
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{train},\text{tree}}^{l}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{train},\text{tree}}^{l}\coloneqq\left\{{\mathcal{T}}\mid{\mathcal{T}}\text{ is a perfect $k$-ary tree with distinct nodes in $[N]$ and depth $l$}\right\},

(17)

and define
p_{\text{train,tree}}^{l}={\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\text{train},\text{tree}}^{l})
as the uniform distribution on
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{train},\text{tree}}^{l}
.
We assume, for any given tree
{\mathcal{T}}
with any depth
l\in[L]
, the environment generates the goal node
g({\mathcal{T}})
according to a latent
goal distribution

p^{a}=(p^{a}_{1},\cdots,p^{a}_{k})\in\Delta({\mathcal{A}}_{\texttt{down}})

that is unknown to the agent. Specifically, after a tree
{\mathcal{T}}
is generated from
p_{\text{train,tree}}^{l}
, the environment takes action
a^{\text{env}}\overset{i.i.d}{\sim}p^{a}
at each node – starting from the root – until a leaf is reached and assigned as a goal. Let
p^{a}(\cdot\mid{\mathcal{T}})
denote the goal distribution induced by
p^{a}
given tree
{\mathcal{T}}
. We define the training distribution
{\mathcal{P}}_{l}
of trees with depth
l\in[L]
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{l}({\mathcal{T}},g)=p_{\text{train,tree}}^{l}({\mathcal{T}})p^{a}(g\mid{\mathcal{T}}).

(18)

Here, we focus on the case where the goal distribution
p^{a}
is balanced, i.e., we assume

\displaystyle\text{(balanced goal:)}\quad\quad p^{a}_{1}=p^{a}_{2}=\cdots=p^{a}_{k}=\frac{1}{k}.

(19)

In Section
4
, we also consider the case where the goal distribution
p^{a}
is imbalanced.

Training with policy gradient.

The construction in Theorem
1
suggests that the last row of
P
can be fixed to 0, so for simplicity and consistency with our construction, we let

\displaystyle P=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{P}\\
0_{1\times d_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

(20)

where
\overline{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d_{2}}
, and set trainable parameters to be
\theta=\{B,C,\overline{P},Q\}
. We train the policy of the form (
11
) with policy gradient. Specifically, we let
\theta^{(t)}=\{B^{(t)},C^{(t)},\overline{P}^{(t)},Q^{(t)}\}
denote the parameters at iteration
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and initialize all matrices to be zero matrices, i.e.,

\displaystyle B^{(0)}=0_{d_{1}\times d_{1}},\quad C^{(0)}=0_{d_{3}\times d_{3}},\quad\overline{P}^{(0)}=0_{k\times d_{2}},\quad Q^{(0)}=0_{(k+1)\times d_{3}}.

(21)

We write
J(\theta)
as the shorthand of
J^{\pi_{\theta}}({\mathcal{P}})
when the training distribution
{\mathcal{P}}
is clear from the context.
Then under our setting, the policy gradient update at iteration
t
is given by

\displaystyle\theta^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\theta^{(t)}+\eta\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta^{(t)})

\displaystyle=\theta^{(t)}+\eta{\mathbb{E}}_{\Phi\sim{\mathcal{P}},\pi_{\theta}}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a_{h}\mid E^{(h)})\sum_{i=h}^{H}r_{i+1}\right],

(22)

where the second equality follows from the policy gradient theorem
(
21
;
17
)
, and
\eta>0
is the learning rate.

3.2
Depth-wise Curriculum Training

We aim to demonstrate that after training on trees of depth 1 and 2 well enough, the agent learns the DFS algorithm and can perform well on deeper trees. Specifically, we use a two-stage training with curriculum schedulers
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in(0,1)
:

(i)

depth-1 curriculum training (training step
t\in[T_{1}]
): we first train on depth-1 trees
{\mathcal{T}}\sim{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
;

(ii)

depth-2 curriculum training (training step
T_{1}+1\leq t\leq T_{2}
): as soon as the success rate
R(\pi;{\mathcal{P}}_{1})
reaches
1-\delta_{1}
, we
start training on depth-2 trees
{\mathcal{T}}\sim{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
until
R(\pi;{\mathcal{P}}_{2})
reaches
1-\delta_{2}
.

To simplify the analysis, we make the following orthonormality assumption on the embedding matrices defined in (
12
).

Assumption 1

(orthonormal embeddings)
.

The matrices
U,V,Z
each have orthonormal columns.

Theoretical guarantees.

We now show that after training with proper choices of
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
for sufficiently long, the trained policy can succeed
on an arbitrary full
k
-ary tree with depth
l\in[L]
that goes significantly beyond the training depth. The formal versions (for both Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
) and their proofs are in Appendix
A
.

Theorem 2

(Depth-1 curriculum training, informal)
.

Suppose the goal node is balanced (cf. (
19
)), Assumption
1
holds,

k,N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
are sufficiently large, and
\epsilon,\eta>0
are sufficiently small,
\delta_{1}=\epsilon^{1.05}
. For depth-1 curriculum training, the success rate
R(\pi;{\mathcal{P}}_{1})
reaches
1-\delta_{1}
in
T_{1}=O(1/(\eta\epsilon^{1.1}))
iterations. After the training, we have

\displaystyle R(\pi^{(T_{1})};{\mathcal{P}}_{l})=O\left(1/k^{l-1}\right),\quad\forall 2\leq l\leq L.

(23)

Theorem
2
shows that after training on depth-1 trees, the transformer can achieve arbitrary accuracy on depth-1 trees, yet its generalization performance on deeper trees still degenerates exponentially. Examining the training dynamics, by time
T_{1}
, the matrices
A_{B}
,
A_{C}
, and
A_{Q}
are close to their respective constructions in Theorem
1
, but generalization still fails because
A_{Q}
remains inadequately trained: after exhausting the children of a non-root internal node, the agent does not move up with high probability.
We therefore freeze the weight matrices
B
,
C
,
Q
and train only
\overline{P}
on depth-2 trees to learn the remaining DFS behavior. The outcome of this stage is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 3

(Depth-2 curriculum training and depth generalization, informal)
.

Suppose the same assumptions in Theorem
2
hold, and fix
\delta_{2}=\epsilon^{1.02}/k^{L}
. For depth-2 curriculum training, the success rate
R(\pi;{\mathcal{P}}_{2})
reaches
1-\delta_{2}
in
T_{2}-T_{1}=O\left(k^{L}/(\eta\epsilon^{1.1})\right)
iterations.
After the training, given any full
k
-ary tree
{\mathcal{T}}
with depth
l\in[L]
and any goal node
g({\mathcal{T}})
, we have

\displaystyle R(\pi^{(T_{2})};({\mathcal{T}},g({\mathcal{T}})))\geq 1-\epsilon.

(24)

Theorem
3
establishes that after training on depth-2 trees, the learned policy generalize to trees with arbitrary depth and goal node by implementing DFS, where all weight matrices converge to their construction in Theorem
1
. While the training distribution is confined to perfect
k
-ary trees to exploit symmetry, the learned policy is applicable to any full
k
-ary trees. Compared with existing analyses on prompt-revealed or fixed graphs
(
12
;
19
;
20
)
, or RL/RLVR over generated traces
(
2
;
6
)
, our hidden-environment agentic setting requires tracking a multi-phase interaction among the induced matrices and return-to-go estimates; see Appendix
A.1
for discussions.

4
Extension to Imbalanced Goal Distribution

So far we have focused on balanced goal distributions (c.f. (
19
)). In practice, goals can be imbalanced: for instance, in web search, desired information is usually more likely to appear among top-ranked results. This suggests that it is beneficial to prioritize the search over more promising branches according to some unknown non-uniform prior
(
8
)
. We formalize the additional prior information via an averaged full-tree environment distribution
{\mathcal{P}}
. For any
l\in[L]
, let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{env},\text{tree}}^{l}\coloneqq\left\{{\mathcal{T}}\mid{\mathcal{T}}\text{ is a full $k$-ary tree with distinct nodes in $[N]$ and depth }l\right\},

(25)

and let
p_{\text{env},\text{tree}}^{l}={\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\text{env},\text{tree}}^{l})
be the uniform distribution on
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{env},\text{tree}}^{l}
. Similar to the balanced setting, given a sampled tree
{\mathcal{T}}
, the environment generates
g({\mathcal{T}})
by sampling latent actions independently from

p^{a}=(p^{a}_{1},\ldots,p^{a}_{k})

from the root until a leaf is reached, where we index the actions by decreasing likelihood:

\displaystyle\text{(imbalanced goal:)}\quad\quad p^{a}_{1}>p^{a}_{2}>\cdots>p^{a}_{k}>0.

(26)

Denoting the induced distribution by
p^{a}(\cdot\mid{\mathcal{T}})
, define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}:={\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}},g)=p_{\text{env},\text{tree}}({\mathcal{T}})\,p^{a}(g\mid{\mathcal{T}}),\,\,\text{ where }\,\,p_{\text{env},\text{tree}}({\mathcal{T}})\coloneqq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\omega_{l}\,p_{\text{env},\text{tree}}^{l}({\mathcal{T}})

(27)

as the environment distribution, where the weights
\omega_{l}>0
and
\sum_{l=1}^{L}\omega_{l}=1
.

Benefit of discounting.

Define

\displaystyle\Pi_{\text{success}}\coloneqq\Pi_{\text{success}}({\mathcal{P}})\coloneqq\left\{\pi:R(\pi;{\mathcal{P}})=1\right\},

(28)

which is the set of policies that find the goal almost surely under
{\mathcal{P}}
.
Because
\Pi_{\text{success}}
contains infinitely many policies, which cannot be distinguished by the undiscounted objective, we consider maximizing the
discounted
expected return, i.e.,

\displaystyle J_{\gamma}^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{1}\sim\rho,a_{h}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid s_{h}),\atop s_{h+1}\sim{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\mid s_{h},a_{h}),h\in[H]}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\gamma^{h-1}r_{h+1}\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{\Phi\sim{\mathcal{P}},\pi}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\gamma^{h-1}r_{h+1}\right],

(29)

where
\gamma\in(0,1]
is the discount factor. We further define the expected path length of policy
\pi
as
H^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})
, which provides a quantitative means to distinguish the policies in
\Pi_{\text{success}}
. Since we only provide a terminal reward, shorter path lengths result in higher discounted expected returns. Proposition
1
shows that in the balanced case, every policy in
\Pi_{\text{success}}
has the same expected path length, so any successful DFS policy, including random DFS, is optimal.

Proposition 1

(optimal policy under balanced goal distribution)
.

Under balanced goal distribution (
19
), any policy
\pi\in\Pi_{\text{success}}
has the same expected path length
H^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})
.

In contrast, the imbalanced case has a unique
ranked DFS
policy that minimizes the expected path length among successful policies (up to behavior on unreachable histories):

Definition 1

(Ranked DFS)
.

Under the imbalanced goal distribution (
26
), at any reachable, non-terminal state, if the current node has unvisited children, the
ranked DFS
policy
\pi_{\mathsf{ranked}}^{\star}
explores them in decreasing order of
p^{a}_{j}
; otherwise, it takes action
up
.

Moreover, for a suitable
\gamma<1
, maximizing
J_{\gamma}^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})
exactly recovers this policy, formalized in Proposition
2
.

Proposition 2

(optimal policy under imbalanced goal distribution)
.

Under the imbalanced goal distribution (
26
), we have
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi_{\textnormal{success}}
, and

1.

H^{\pi^{\star}}({\mathcal{P}})<H^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})
for any
\pi\in\Pi_{\text{success}}
whose reachable behavior differs from
\pi_{\mathsf{ranked}}^{\star}
.

2.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that for any
\gamma\in\Big(1-C\left(p^{a}_{k}\right)^{L},1\Big)
,
\pi^{\star}=\argmax_{\pi}J_{\gamma}^{\pi}({\mathcal{P}})
up to reachable behavior.

The proof of both propositions is postponed to Appendix
B
. We also call
\pi^{\star}
the
optimal policy
for the imbalanced case.

Transformer construction.

The ranked DFS policy can also be implemented by our two-head shallow transformer.
Theorem
4
modifies the induced matrices in (
1
) by replacing the uniform row weights in
A_{Q}
with a priority vector
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, yielding ranked DFS
\pi^{\star}
. The proof is provided in Appendix
C.2
.

Theorem 4

(Transformer construction for ranked DFS)
.

Assume
U,V,Z
all have full column rank. Then there exists
\theta=\{B,C,P,Q\}
such that

\displaystyle A_{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&a_{b,0}1_{1\times N}\\
0_{N\times 1}&a_{b,1}I_{N}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad A_{C}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{c,0}&-a_{c,1}&0\\
-a_{c,0}&a_{c,1}&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle A_{P}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0_{k\times 1}&-a_{p,1}I_{k}&0_{k\times 1}\\
0&0_{1\times k}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad A_{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0_{k\times 1}&0_{k\times 1}\\
-a_{q,0}&a_{q,\times}&0\end{pmatrix}

(30)

for any
a_{b,0},a_{b,1},a_{c,0},a_{c,1},a_{q,0},a_{q,\times},a_{p,1}\in\mathbb{R}
, any
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. For any
k\geq 2
,
L\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,
N\geq\frac{k^{L+1}-1}{k-1}
and precision
\epsilon>0
, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that when

\displaystyle e^{a_{c,0}}

\displaystyle\geq\ln k,\quad e^{a_{c,1}}\geq\sqrt{Nk}\ln k,\quad e^{a_{b,0}}\geq Nk\ln k,\quad e^{a_{b,1}}\geq\ln ke^{a_{b,0}},

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}

\displaystyle\asymp a_{q,0}\asymp a_{q,\times}\gtrsim\lambda_{k}\geq 0,\quad\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1},\,a_{q,0},\,a_{q,\times}\geq C\ln\left(N/\epsilon\right),\,\,\forall i\in[k-1],

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p,1}}{k}

\displaystyle\asymp\lambda_{1},\quad\frac{a_{p,1}}{k}\geq C\left(\ln\left(N/\epsilon\right)+a_{q,0}+\lambda_{1}\right),

(31)

for any
\Phi=({\mathcal{T}},g({\mathcal{T}}))
where
{\mathcal{T}}
is a full
k
-ary tree with depth
l\in[L]
, and
g({\mathcal{T}})
is an arbitrary leaf, and any reachable non-terminal state
s_{h}
, we have

\displaystyle R(\pi_{\theta};\Phi)\geq 1-\epsilon,\quad\left\|\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid s_{h})-\pi^{\star}(\cdot\mid s_{h})\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2N}.

(32)

Our experiments in Section
5
show that discounted-return training recovers
\pi^{\star}
in the imbalanced setting and learns induced matrices similar to the construction above.

5
Numerical Experiments

Setup.

To validate our training analysis, we conduct simulations using the transformer defined in (
11
) with zero initialization of the parameters. We set
k=3
,
N=\frac{k^{L+1}-1}{k-1}+10
,
d_{1}=N+1
,
d_{2}=k+2
,
d_{3}=3
, and choose
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
,
v_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,
z_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}}
to be the one-hot vectors with respect to the index
i
,
j
, and
p
respectively (assuming the index starts from 0 here). For both balanced and imbalanced cases, we follow the two-depth curriculum training procedure, and use stochastic policy gradient with batch size
b=256
to train the model, i.e., we have

\displaystyle\theta^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\theta^{(t)}+\frac{\eta}{b}\sum_{j=1}^{b}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}\left(a_{h}^{(j)}\mid E^{(h,j)}\right)\sum_{i=h}^{H}\gamma^{i-1}r_{i+1}^{(j)},

(33)

where
E^{(h,j)}
(
j\in[b]
) is the state embedding of the state
s_{h}^{(j)}
at the
h
-th step in the
j
-th trajectory in the batch.
Each trajectory
(s_{1}^{(j)},a_{1}^{(j)},\dots,s_{H}^{(j)},a_{H}^{(j)})
is obtained by first sampling a tree
{\mathcal{T}}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}p_{\text{train,tree}}^{l}
(
l=1,2
) from the training distribution and then executing
\pi_{\theta}
starting from the root node, until the episode ends.
We set the learning rate
\eta=10
.

•

For the balanced case, we set
L=4
,
\gamma=1
, and train
\theta=\{B,C,\overline{P},Q\}
at the depth-1 stage for 1000 iterations, and train
\theta=\{B,\overline{P}\}
at the depth-2 stage for 49600 iterations.
2
2

2

For faster convergence, we also train
B
at depth-2 stage in the experiments.

•

For the imbalanced case, we set
L=3
,
\gamma=0.9
,
p^{a}_{i+1}=0.9p^{a}_{i},i\in[k-1]
, and train
\theta=\{B,C,P,Q\}
at both depths for 1100 and 50000 iterations, respectively.

For every 50 iterations, we also evaluate on
L
test sets, each of size
128
, consisting of i.i.d. random full
k
-ary trees with depth
l=1,\cdots,L
respectively.
